- PubMed: Gratitude and Relationship Maintenance in Intimate Bonds
- Gottman: Sound Relationship House Theory
18. Q: What characterizes the negativistic interaction pattern described in Chapter 7?
A: Show answer
The negativistic pattern emphasizes mistakes and overlooks positive contributions. A person using this pattern may criticize missed tasks, highlight shortcomings, or say that successful behavior is merely what should be expected, while rarely acknowledging effort or achievement. Over time, the other person may feel that nothing they do is noticed unless it is wrong. This can create resentment and reduce motivation to contribute. The corrective approach is not to stop giving necessary feedback; it is to make feedback specific and proportionate while also recognizing genuine effort, improvement, and helpful behavior.
Study instruction:
- "For one week, notice whether your feedback disproportionately highlights errors and identify specific positive contributions that also deserve acknowledgment."
Key point:
- Ignoring positive contributions can make a relationship feel persistently critical.
- Correction and sincere recognition should coexist.
Source links:
- The Like Switch — official publisher page
- PubMed: Gratitude and Relationship Maintenance in Intimate Bonds
19. Q: What is the perfectionistic interaction pattern, and how can it undermine a relationship?
A: Show answer
The perfectionistic pattern sets standards so high that competent performance rarely receives approval. The person may focus on minor flaws, insist on unnecessary refinements, or treat an adequate result as unacceptable because it is not ideal. This can make ordinary shared tasks exhausting and create a sense that success is always out of reach. The remedy is to distinguish essential standards from preferences, safety requirements, or cosmetic improvements. Some tasks genuinely require exacting standards, but many everyday activities can be completed competently without pursuing perfection at any cost. Expectations should be discussed and proportionate to the task.
Study instruction:
- "Select a recurring task and distinguish its essential requirements from optional refinements that add little practical value."
Key point:
- Unreasonable standards can turn ordinary cooperation into a source of chronic frustration.
- Competence is often sufficient when perfection adds little meaningful benefit.
Source links:
- The Like Switch — official publisher page
- PubMed: Gratitude and Relationship Maintenance in Intimate Bonds
20. Q: What is the all-or-nothing punishment pattern described in the chapter?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes a pattern in which one mistake cancels out much or all of the praise accumulated through many positive actions. This creates an unfair scorekeeping system: good behavior is treated as temporary or irrelevant, while a single error becomes the defining event. The chapter labels this pattern with a deliberately severe term, but that label should not be treated as a clinical diagnosis. The practical issue is disproportionate negative weighting. A fair response considers the seriousness, context, and recurrence of the mistake while retaining an accurate view of the person's broader behavior.
Study instruction:
- "Review a disagreement and assess whether one mistake was allowed to erase a longer pattern of positive contribution without justification."
Key point:
- A single error should not automatically invalidate a history of constructive behavior.
- Feedback should be proportionate to the seriousness and pattern of the conduct.
Source links:
- The Like Switch — official publisher page
- PubMed: Gratitude and Relationship Maintenance in Intimate Bonds
- American Psychological Association: Strategies for Controlling Anger
21. Q: Why is positive attention important even after people have been together for many years?
A: Show answer
Positive attention communicates that the other person is still noticed and valued. As relationships become familiar, partners may assume that appreciation is understood and stop expressing it. The result can be a relationship dominated by logistics, corrections, and requests. Sincere appreciation counters that drift by identifying specific actions or qualities that matter. Research on gratitude in intimate relationships has linked feeling appreciated with greater responsiveness to a partner's needs and stronger commitment, although the findings describe associations and processes rather than a guarantee. Positive attention is most useful when it is genuine, timely, and not conditional on receiving something in return.
Study instruction:
- "Give one specific, truthful expression of appreciation and explain exactly what action or quality you value."
Key point:
- People do not outgrow the need to feel appreciated.
- Specific, sincere appreciation can reinforce responsiveness and connection.
Source links:
- The Like Switch — official publisher page
- PubMed: Gratitude and Relationship Maintenance in Intimate Bonds
- Gottman: Sound Relationship House Theory
22. Q: How should praise be given so that it strengthens rather than manipulates a relationship?
A: Show answer
Praise should identify a real contribution, effort, quality, or improvement and communicate why it mattered. It should not be exaggerated, offered sarcastically, or used as a disguised attempt to obtain compliance. For example, saying that a partner's careful planning made a difficult week easier is more informative than offering a vague compliment immediately before asking for a favor. Praise should also leave room for the person to receive it naturally; some people prefer private acknowledgment rather than public attention. The credibility of praise depends on honesty and consistency, not on its frequency alone.
Study instruction:
- "Rewrite a generic compliment as a specific statement that identifies the action and its positive effect without attaching a request."
Key point:
- Sincere praise recognizes something real and specific.
- Compliments lose credibility when used as a bargaining device.
Source links:
- The Like Switch — official publisher page
- PubMed: Gratitude and Relationship Maintenance in Intimate Bonds
23. Q: Why should important milestones and ordinary acts of kindness be acknowledged?
A: Show answer
Acknowledging birthdays, anniversaries, achievements, personal milestones, and small thoughtful acts signals that the other person's experiences matter. The gesture need not be expensive. A personal message, a remembered preference, a quiet celebration, or an explicit thank-you may be more meaningful than a costly but impersonal gift. The important element is attention: the person has noticed what matters and taken time to respond. Milestones should be recognized in ways that fit the recipient's preferences, because some people enjoy public celebration while others prefer privacy or simplicity.
Study instruction:
- "List three milestones or small acts that matter to someone close to you and identify a respectful way to acknowledge each one."
Key point:
- Recognition communicates attention and appreciation.
- The form of recognition should fit the recipient's preferences.
Source links:
- The Like Switch — official publisher page
- PubMed: Gratitude and Relationship Maintenance in Intimate Bonds
24. Q: Why should partners participate together in major decisions that affect them both?
A: Show answer
Joint decision-making gives each person an opportunity to contribute relevant information, express concerns, and understand the reasons for the final choice. Decisions about finances, major purchases, relocation, work changes, health, caregiving, and family responsibilities can affect both people even when one person has more technical knowledge. Consultation often increases understanding and ownership, but it does not mean every decision must be unanimous or that one partner should be pressured into agreement. The parties should clarify which choices require joint consent, which involve delegated responsibility, and how disagreements will be resolved.
Study instruction:
- "Choose a shared decision and identify each person's interests, information needs, decision rights, and agreed method for reaching a conclusion."
Key point:
- Consultation builds understanding and shared responsibility.
- Joint participation must preserve genuine choice and clearly defined decision rights.
Source links:
- The Like Switch — official publisher page
- Gottman: Sound Relationship House Theory
25. Q: When is public recognition appropriate, and when might it be uncomfortable?
A: Show answer
Public recognition can communicate pride and appreciation, especially when a partner values being acknowledged in front of others. However, it can embarrass someone who is private, introverted, or uncomfortable with attention. The giver should consider the recipient's preferences, the setting, the nature of the achievement, and whether the recognition could reveal private information. A tasteful acknowledgment is more likely to be welcomed than exaggerated praise that makes the recipient feel exposed. If uncertain, ask privately how the person prefers accomplishments to be recognized.
Study instruction:
- "Ask someone how they prefer achievements to be acknowledged and compare their preference with your usual approach."
Key point:
- Recognition is meaningful only when it respects the recipient's comfort and privacy.
- Public praise should be tailored rather than assumed to be universally welcome.
Source links:
- The Like Switch — official publisher page
- PubMed: Gratitude and Relationship Maintenance in Intimate Bonds
26. Q: How can a partner avoid giving a reward or gift that misses the recipient's actual preferences?
A: Show answer
Good intentions do not guarantee that a gift or gesture will be appreciated. People may value different experiences, objects, forms of help, or ways of spending time. The most reliable approach is to listen for stated preferences, ask questions when appropriate, and observe what the person repeatedly chooses or values. A surprise can still be preserved by agreeing on a shortlist of acceptable options or keeping a record of ideas the recipient has volunteered. The objective is not to make the giver look generous; it is to respond to what the recipient genuinely enjoys.
Study instruction:
- "Create a preference-checking method that preserves some surprise while ensuring that gifts and gestures reflect the recipient's expressed interests."
Key point:
- A gift should reflect the recipient's preferences, not only the giver's intentions.
- Asking and listening are more reliable than guessing.
Source links:
- The Like Switch — official publisher page
- Utah State University Extension: Using Active Listening to Enhance Your Relationships
27. Q: How can the chapter's CARE elements work together in a practical interaction?
A: Show answer
Consider a partner who has had a difficult day. Compassion begins by recognizing that the situation matters and offering support. Active listening means giving the person time to explain what happened, without interrupting or immediately proposing a solution. Empathy involves reflecting the concern accurately, such as recognizing that the person feels disappointed because an important effort was overlooked. Reinforcement may involve acknowledging how thoughtfully the person handled a difficult responsibility or thanking them for explaining what they need. The sequence is not rigid; people may need practical help before discussing feelings, or may prefer quiet company. The listener should ask what would be useful rather than assume.
Study instruction:
- "Role-play a difficult-day conversation and demonstrate at least one observable behavior for each CARE element."
Key point:
- CARE is most effective when its elements are integrated rather than performed mechanically.
- Ask what support is wanted instead of assuming the same response works for everyone.
Source links:
- The Like Switch — official publisher page
- Utah State University Extension: Using Active Listening to Enhance Your Relationships
- PubMed: Gratitude and Relationship Maintenance in Intimate Bonds
28. Q: Why is anger a normal part of relationships, and when does it become harmful?
A: Show answer
Anger is a normal human emotion that may signal frustration, hurt, perceived unfairness, fear, or a boundary concern. Its presence does not automatically mean that a relationship is unhealthy. The risk depends on how anger is expressed and what behavior follows. Uncontrolled anger can lead to insults, threats, intimidation, aggression, impulsive decisions, or prolonged hostility. It can also be suppressed until it emerges indirectly through withdrawal or resentment. The useful goal is not to eliminate anger but to understand its message, regulate the response, and address the underlying issue without harming another person.
Study instruction:
- "Identify the concern beneath an angry reaction and distinguish the emotion itself from the behavior chosen in response."
Key point:
- Anger is an emotion; harmful conduct is a behavioral choice that requires accountability.
- The aim is constructive regulation, not denial of legitimate concerns.
Source links:
- The Like Switch — official publisher page
- American Psychological Association: Strategies for Controlling Anger
- Mayo Clinic: Anger Management
29. Q: Why can highly escalated anger make problem-solving more difficult?
A: Show answer
When people feel threatened or overwhelmed, attention may narrow and they may become more focused on defending themselves than evaluating information. They may interrupt, interpret neutral comments as hostile, or struggle to consider alternatives. The chapter describes this through the fight-or-flight response. That description is a useful simplified explanation, not a rule that every angry person loses all reasoning ability or that a fixed biological timer applies to everyone. The practical lesson is to notice when the conversation is no longer productive and reduce intensity before attempting complex negotiation or detailed explanations.
Study instruction:
- "Identify observable signs that a discussion has become unproductive and decide what pause or safety step is appropriate."
Key point:
- High emotional arousal can make careful listening and reasoning harder.
- Do not assume every person responds to anger in the same way.
Source links:
- The Like Switch — official publisher page
- American Psychological Association: Strategies for Controlling Anger
- Mayo Clinic: Anger Management
30. Q: Why should someone avoid arguing logically with a person who is highly escalated?
A: Show answer
A detailed explanation may be ineffective when the other person is overwhelmed, because they may hear it as dismissal, excuse-making, or another challenge. Repeating facts more loudly rarely improves understanding. A better first response is to lower the intensity: listen, acknowledge the concern without endorsing inaccurate claims, and allow a pause if needed. Once the person is more settled, the parties can examine evidence, explain constraints, and consider solutions. This does not require tolerating abuse or surrendering a legitimate boundary. If the interaction becomes threatening, the priority is to disengage safely and seek appropriate help.
Study instruction:
- "Prepare a short response that acknowledges an angry person's concern while postponing detailed problem-solving until both parties can participate constructively."
Key point:
- Timing affects whether explanations can be heard.
- De-escalation does not require abandoning facts, boundaries, or safety.
Source links:
- The Like Switch — official publisher page
- American Psychological Association: Strategies for Controlling Anger
- Mayo Clinic: Anger Management
31. Q: How should a cooling-off period be used without turning it into avoidance?
A: Show answer
A cooling-off period should be a purposeful pause that helps participants regain composure and return to the issue. The person requesting the pause can explain that they want to avoid saying something harmful, propose a reasonable time to resume, and identify any urgent action that cannot wait. During the pause, they can breathe slowly, take a walk, reflect on the actual issue, or write down questions. The chapter mentions an approximate period for physiological recovery, but people differ; the return should depend on readiness and safety rather than a universal minute count. If one person repeatedly refuses to resume, the issue may need a structured conversation or professional support.
Study instruction:
- "Write a pause-and-return agreement that includes a reason for the pause, a check-in time, and a plan for urgent matters."
Key point:
- A pause should create conditions for a better conversation, not erase the problem.
- Readiness varies; use agreed return arrangements rather than a rigid timer.
Source links:
- The Like Switch — official publisher page
- American Psychological Association: Strategies for Controlling Anger
- Mayo Clinic: Anger Management
32. Q: When can a simple explanation reduce anger, and what makes an explanation ineffective?
A: Show answer
An explanation may reduce anger when the other person lacks relevant information and the explanation is concise, credible, and directly connected to the concern. For example, a delay caused by a missing dependency may be easier to understand when the dependency and the steps taken are explained. An explanation becomes ineffective when it sounds like blame-shifting, omits the speaker's own responsibility, contradicts available evidence, or is delivered before the other person feels heard. The speaker should acknowledge the impact, provide verifiable context, and state what will be done next. An explanation is not a substitute for correcting a failure or apologizing when responsibility is established.
Study instruction:
- "Prepare a brief explanation of a missed commitment that acknowledges impact, states verified facts, and identifies corrective action."
Key point:
- Explanations work best when they clarify rather than excuse.
- Credibility requires acknowledging one's own responsibility where it exists.
Source links:
- The Like Switch — official publisher page
- American Psychological Association: Strategies for Controlling Anger
33. Q: What are the three components of the chapter's Big Three approach to breaking the anger cycle?
A: Show answer
The chapter identifies empathic statements, venting, and presumptive statements. An empathic statement reflects the concern or feeling the speaker appears to be expressing. Venting gives the person an opportunity to explain frustration rather than having the listener immediately debate or dismiss it. A presumptive statement proposes a constructive next step based on what has been heard. The sequence is intended to move from understanding toward action. It should be applied flexibly and ethically: the listener must check whether the interpretation is accurate, allow the other person to disagree, and avoid using a proposed action to corner or manipulate them.
Study instruction:
- "Create a three-step practice dialogue showing an accurate reflection, an opportunity to explain, and a mutually acceptable next step."
Key point:
- Empathy and listening precede problem-solving in the chapter's model.
- A proposed next step should remain open to discussion and consent.
Source links:
- The Like Switch — official publisher page
- Utah State University Extension: Using Active Listening to Enhance Your Relationships
- American Psychological Association: Strategies for Controlling Anger
34. Q: How can someone identify the underlying concern behind another person's anger?
A: Show answer
The listener should look beyond the volume or intensity of the emotion and examine what the person says they need, fear, value, or believe has been threatened. Clues may include repeated phrases, specific events, changes in tone, and the consequences the person describes. The listener can then test an interpretation with a question such as, "Is the main concern that you were left to handle this alone?" This is more useful than merely announcing that the person is angry. Anger can arise from several overlapping causes, so the listener should remain open to correction rather than assume that one interpretation explains everything.
Study instruction:
- "Take an example of anger and identify at least two possible underlying concerns before asking the speaker which, if either, is accurate."
Key point:
- Anger may be a signal of a deeper concern rather than the whole issue.
- Treat interpretations as hypotheses to check, not facts about another person's mind.
Source links:
- The Like Switch — official publisher page
- Utah State University Extension: Using Active Listening to Enhance Your Relationships
- American Psychological Association: Strategies for Controlling Anger
35. Q: How should an empathic statement be constructed?
A: Show answer
An effective empathic statement reflects the specific concern or meaning that appears to be important to the speaker. It may refer to the event, the impact, or the value involved: for example, "You are frustrated because the change was made without consulting you." The statement should be concise and delivered without sarcasm, accusation, or an exaggerated emotional label. The speaker must be allowed to correct it. If the reflection is inaccurate, the listener should ask what they missed and try again. Empathy is not agreement with every claim; it is an effort to understand the person's experience accurately enough to respond respectfully.
Study instruction:
- "Write three empathic reflections that name the situation and likely impact, then add a question inviting correction."
Key point:
- Specific reflections are more useful than merely naming an obvious emotion.
- The person whose experience is being reflected has the final say on whether the reflection fits.
Source links:
- The Like Switch — official publisher page
- Utah State University Extension: Using Active Listening to Enhance Your Relationships
36. Q: What is the purpose of venting during a difficult conversation?
A: Show answer
Venting gives a frustrated person space to describe what happened, why it matters, and what they believe has been overlooked. A listener can support this process by remaining attentive, using brief acknowledgments, and asking clarifying questions without turning the exchange into an interrogation. Venting is not permission for threats, abuse, repeated humiliation, or unlimited monologues that prevent the other person from speaking. The listener can set boundaries while still acknowledging the concern. Once the person has expressed the central issue, the conversation can move toward facts, needs, and possible action.
Study instruction:
- "Practice listening to a complaint for several minutes and summarize the main issue without interrupting or endorsing personal attacks."
Key point:
- Being heard can reduce the pressure to repeat a concern.
- Venting should remain respectful and bounded; it is not a license for abuse.
Source links:
- The Like Switch — official publisher page
- Utah State University Extension: Using Active Listening to Enhance Your Relationships
- American Psychological Association: Strategies for Controlling Anger
37. Q: How can a listener tell whether emotional intensity is decreasing?
A: Show answer
A person may show signs such as a slower pace of speech, fewer repeated accusations, longer pauses, relaxed posture, a quieter voice, or willingness to consider a question. The chapter mentions sighs and lowered shoulders as possible signs that anger has subsided. These cues are not definitive: a sigh may indicate exhaustion, frustration, or something else, and quietness may reflect withdrawal rather than calm. The listener should check directly, for example by asking whether the person feels ready to consider next steps. If the answer is no, continue listening or agree on a later time.
Study instruction:
- "List observable changes in a scenario and formulate a neutral question to check whether the person is ready to problem-solve."
Key point:
- Behavioral cues can suggest a change in intensity but do not prove emotional readiness.
- Ask before moving from listening to solutions.
Source links:
- The Like Switch — official publisher page
- Utah State University Extension: Using Active Listening to Enhance Your Relationships
- Mayo Clinic: Anger Management
38. Q: What is a presumptive statement, and how can it be used ethically?
A: Show answer
In the chapter, a presumptive statement proposes a course of action that appears to address the concern the person has just expressed. For example, after hearing that someone is overwhelmed by shared responsibilities, a partner might offer to take a specific task and ask whether that would help. Used ethically, the proposal is transparent, realistic, and open to refusal or revision. It should not be designed to make disagreement seem unreasonable or to exploit a person's emotions. The listener should confirm the person's actual needs and make sure the proposed action respects their autonomy. A collaborative question is generally safer than a forced conclusion.
Study instruction:
- "Convert a directive proposal into a transparent offer that gives the other person a genuine opportunity to accept, decline, or suggest an alternative."
Key point:
- A useful proposal connects to the concern that was actually expressed.
- Persuasion must not become coercion or a false choice.
Source links:
- The Like Switch — official publisher page
- American Psychological Association: Strategies for Controlling Anger
- Gottman: Steps for Handling Conflict Constructively
39. Q: What should someone do if the other person rejects a proposed solution?
A: Show answer
Rejection may mean that the person has not finished explaining the concern, that the proposed solution does not address the real problem, or that additional information is needed. The listener should not repeat the proposal more forcefully or treat refusal as irrational. Instead, ask what remains unresolved, reflect the new information, and revise the options together. Sometimes the parties may need to acknowledge that they disagree, set a boundary, or seek help from a neutral professional. A solution is meaningful only if it addresses the problem and can be accepted without pressure.
Study instruction:
- "Prepare follow-up questions for a rejected proposal that explore what is missing and invite a different solution."
Key point:
- A rejected proposal is information about fit, timing, or unresolved concerns.
- Respectful negotiation allows revision and refusal.
Source links:
- The Like Switch — official publisher page
- Utah State University Extension: Using Active Listening to Enhance Your Relationships
- Gottman: Steps for Handling Conflict Constructively
40. Q: How should the two-option technique be adapted to preserve genuine choice?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes presenting two options so an angry person can choose a path forward. In practice, the options must be real, lawful, clearly explained, and not misleading. The listener should disclose relevant constraints and, where possible, invite the person to suggest another permissible option. Presenting a false choice or pretending that a person controls an outcome they cannot control is manipulative. For example, an official may explain the available legal routes and consequences, but should not misrepresent the person's rights. In personal relationships, options should be negotiated rather than used to pressure a partner into compliance.
Study instruction:
- "Review a two-option proposal and test whether both options are genuine, accurate, and open to a reasonable alternative."
Key point:
- Choice supports autonomy only when the options and constraints are presented honestly.
- Do not manufacture an illusion of control.
Source links:
- The Like Switch — official publisher page
- American Psychological Association: Strategies for Controlling Anger
41. Q: How can a person maintain authority during conflict without intimidating the other party?
A: Show answer
Authority is maintained through clear boundaries, accurate information, consistency, and proportionate action, not through threats or humiliation. A person responsible for enforcing a rule can explain the rule, the reason it exists, the available options, and the consequences of noncompliance. They should remain calm and respectful while applying the same standard consistently. In a personal relationship, authority should not be confused with control over another adult; each person retains autonomy and the right to disagree. Respectful firmness can reduce unnecessary escalation while preserving legitimate responsibilities.
Study instruction:
- "Develop a short explanation of a rule or boundary that is firm, factual, respectful, and clear about the available choices."
Key point:
- Firmness and respect can coexist.
- Legitimate authority should be exercised transparently and proportionately.
Source links:
- The Like Switch — official publisher page
- American Psychological Association: Strategies for Controlling Anger
42. Q: How could the Big Three approach be applied when a supervisor is angry about a late report?
A: Show answer
The employee should first establish the verified cause of the delay and acknowledge the impact on the supervisor's deadline. If the supervisor remains upset, the employee can reflect the underlying concern, such as the risk of being unprepared for a client meeting, and listen for any additional issues. Once the immediate concern is understood, the employee can propose a realistic recovery plan: obtain the missing data, provide an interim version, state a revised delivery time, and communicate any remaining risk. The employee should not blame another department without evidence or promise a deadline that cannot be met. The supervisor should also clarify whether the original deadline and dependencies were realistic.
Study instruction:
- "Write a short dialogue that includes acknowledgment, verified explanation, a recovery plan, and a specific follow-up commitment."
Key point:
- Acknowledge impact before defending the process.
- A credible solution includes ownership, realistic timing, and updates.
Source links:
- The Like Switch — official publisher page
- American Psychological Association: Strategies for Controlling Anger
43. Q: How should a public-facing officer handle an angry visitor who is subject to a legitimate regulatory restriction?
A: Show answer
The officer should remain calm, acknowledge why the item or request matters to the visitor, and explain the applicable rule and its purpose in clear language. The officer should distinguish what is mandatory from what is discretionary, identify any lawful alternatives, and avoid mocking the visitor's beliefs or using misleading pressure. If the visitor continues to object, the officer should follow established procedures, request appropriate supervisory assistance where needed, and protect the safety of everyone present. The chapter's customs example illustrates empathy combined with a firm boundary, but its persuasive dialogue should not be treated as permission to manipulate or misstate legal authority.
Study instruction:
- "Analyze a regulatory dispute and identify the rule, its rationale, any lawful discretion, the available alternatives, and the escalation route."
Key point:
- Empathy does not require abandoning a legitimate legal or safety requirement.
- Explain authority accurately and follow formal procedures.
Source links:
- The Like Switch — official publisher page
- American Psychological Association: Strategies for Controlling Anger
44. Q: How can a person manage their own anger before it harms a relationship?
A: Show answer
Self-management begins with noticing personal warning signs such as tension, faster speech, racing thoughts, raised voice, or an urge to interrupt. The person can pause, breathe slowly, step away briefly, and return when able to state the concern without insults or threats. It also helps to identify recurring triggers, replace absolute accusations such as "always" and "never" with specific observations, and focus on a solvable issue. If anger repeatedly feels uncontrollable or leads to harmful behavior, professional support may be appropriate. Anger management is a skill that can be practiced, not a demand to suppress every feeling.
Study instruction:
- "Create a personal anger plan covering warning signs, a pause strategy, a respectful way to state concerns, and the threshold for seeking help."
Key point:
- Early recognition makes self-regulation easier.
- Repeated harmful anger patterns may require professional support.
Source links:
- American Psychological Association: Strategies for Controlling Anger
- Mayo Clinic: Anger Management

45. Q: When should de-escalation stop and safety become the priority?
A: Show answer
Communication techniques are not a substitute for safety planning. If a person threatens violence, blocks someone from leaving, uses physical force, stalks, coerces, or creates a credible fear of harm, the priority is to move to safety and contact appropriate support rather than continue a persuasion exercise. Empathy does not require staying in a dangerous interaction, and a time-out is not sufficient when there is an immediate threat. In Indonesia, SAPA 129 provides a reporting and support channel for violence against women and children, including a telephone hotline and WhatsApp contact. For immediate danger, use local emergency services and trusted support.
Study instruction:
- "Review the safety indicators in a conflict scenario and identify when to disengage, document safely, and contact a support service."
Key point:
- Safety takes priority over preserving a conversation or relationship.
- Abuse and coercion require protection and appropriate support, not just better communication.

46. Q: What are common reasons long-term relationships may deteriorate even when both people once cared deeply?
A: Show answer
The chapter identifies several possible pressures: diverging interests, children leaving home, a desire for greater independence, changing life priorities, personality changes, infidelity or other third-party disruptions, boredom, and emerging incompatibilities. These factors do not automatically end a relationship, but they can change the expectations and needs that originally held it together. More than one factor may operate at once. The useful response is to identify the actual change rather than assume that every disagreement reflects a lack of love or commitment. Some problems can be addressed through negotiation or counseling; others may reveal a fundamental difference that the parties cannot or do not wish to resolve.
Study instruction:
- "Choose one relationship transition and map the changed needs, expectations, and practical decisions that may follow."
Key point:
- Relationship strain can arise from changing life circumstances as well as individual behavior.
- Identify the actual source of change before deciding how to respond.
Source links:
- The Like Switch — official publisher page
- Australian Psychological Society: When to Seek Help for Relationship Problems
47. Q: How can partners distinguish a temporary life-stage strain from a deeper incompatibility?
A: Show answer
A temporary strain is often linked to a recognizable transition, such as parenting demands, illness, job change, relocation, caregiving, or children leaving home. A deeper incompatibility may involve persistent conflict over core values, expectations, lifestyle, safety, or fundamental life goals. The distinction cannot be made from one argument. Partners should examine duration, recurrence, willingness to negotiate, actual behavior change, and whether each person's essential needs can be respected. It may help to agree on a trial period for specific changes and review whether they improve the situation. If the pattern remains stuck, a qualified relationship professional can help assess the issues.
Study instruction:
- "Compare a temporary stressor with a recurring values conflict and identify evidence that would help distinguish them."
Key point:
- One difficult period does not necessarily define the whole relationship.
- Persistent incompatibility requires honest assessment rather than endless postponement.
Source links:
- The Like Switch — official publisher page
- Australian Psychological Society: When to Seek Help for Relationship Problems
- Gottman: Sound Relationship House Theory
48. Q: When should partners consider professional counseling or relationship support?
A: Show answer
Professional help is worth considering when conflict becomes a repeated pattern, communication narrows, the same issue returns without resolution, trust has been seriously damaged, or the partners cannot identify a workable path forward. A qualified psychologist or relationship counselor can help clarify patterns, improve communication, and assess relevant individual concerns such as substance use or mental-health difficulties. Seeking help does not guarantee reconciliation, and it can also support informed decisions about separation or co-parenting. Where abuse, coercion, or fear is present, safety and specialist support should be assessed before any joint intervention; ordinary communication exercises are not a substitute for protection.
Study instruction:
- "Identify the point at which self-directed conversations are no longer sufficient and list the type of qualified support that may be appropriate."
Key point:
- Repeated unresolved patterns are a reason to seek professional guidance.
- Counseling can support repair, clarity, or a safer transition; it does not guarantee a particular outcome.
Source links:
- Australian Psychological Society: When to Seek Help for Relationship Problems
- Gottman: Sound Relationship House Theory
49. Q: What is the purpose of the chapter's "break glass" letters idea?
A: Show answer
The chapter suggests that partners write private letters early in a relationship describing what they admire and appreciate about one another, seal them, and keep them for a time when conflict or discouragement becomes severe. Reading the letters later may remind the partners of positive qualities and shared reasons for commitment, creating an opening for a calmer discussion. The idea is a symbolic prompt, not a treatment method or a solution to serious misconduct. It is most appropriate when both people freely want to reconnect and the relationship is safe. A letter should never be used to pressure someone to remain in a harmful relationship.
Study instruction:
- "Design a voluntary appreciation-letter exercise and state the circumstances in which using it would be inappropriate."
Key point:
- Remembering positive history may help some couples reconnect.
- A symbolic reminder cannot replace accountability, safety, or substantive problem-solving.
Source links:
- The Like Switch — official publisher page
- PubMed: Gratitude and Relationship Maintenance in Intimate Bonds
- Gottman: Steps for Handling Conflict Constructively
50. Q: How should someone decide whether to continue repairing a relationship or consider ending it?
A: Show answer
The decision should be based on safety, mutual willingness, actual behavior, compatibility, and whether the relationship can meet essential needs without ongoing harm. Repair is more plausible when both people acknowledge problems, accept responsibility, make observable changes, and respect boundaries over time. Repeated promises without behavioral change are not sufficient evidence of repair. A person should not be told to preserve a relationship at any cost. Violence, coercive control, threats, or serious ongoing abuse require safety-focused support and may make separation or protective action necessary. Where the relationship is not abusive but remains persistently unworkable, individual counseling can help clarify options and consequences.
Study instruction:
- "Evaluate a relationship scenario using safety, mutual accountability, observable change, compatibility, and access to support rather than relying only on past investment."
Key point:
- Commitment does not require enduring abuse or sacrificing safety.
- Repair depends on sustained mutual action, not promises or nostalgia alone.

Table 8
Digital Relationships, Online Safety, and Deception Awareness
1. Q: What are the central opportunities and risks of building relationships in a digital world?
A: Show answer
Digital communication can make it easier to meet people, discover shared interests, and begin relationships beyond the limits of geography or immediate social circles. It can be particularly useful for people who prefer time to think before responding or who find it difficult to initiate conversations face to face. At the same time, online interaction can conceal identity, exaggerate personal characteristics, accelerate emotional disclosure, and create a lasting record of information. The chapter’s central position is not to reject online relationships, but to use digital channels deliberately: enjoy their reach and convenience while verifying identity, protecting privacy, pacing intimacy, and remaining alert to manipulation.
Study instruction:
- "Summarize the chapter’s benefits and risks, then explain how verification, privacy, pacing, and judgment address each risk."
Key point:
- Digital platforms expand opportunities for connection but do not guarantee that a person or profile is genuine.
- The safest approach combines openness to relationships with proportionate verification and privacy boundaries.
Source links:
- FTC: Heads Up — Stop, Think, Connect
- FBI: Romance Scams
2. Q: Why can online communication be especially helpful for introverted people?
A: Show answer
Online communication can reduce the pressure of initiating a conversation with a stranger and can give an individual more time to formulate a thoughtful response. A person who is interrupted or overshadowed in a fast-moving group may find it easier to express ideas in writing. Asynchronous messages also allow people to participate at a pace that suits them. These advantages do not mean that every introvert prefers online interaction or that written communication is automatically more authentic. They show that digital channels can remove some situational barriers and create additional routes into social participation.
Study instruction:
- "Compare a written exchange with a face-to-face conversation and identify which features make participation easier or harder for different people."
Key point:
- Digital communication can reduce some social pressures associated with spontaneous conversation.
- Online comfort should complement, not automatically replace, the development of face-to-face relationships.
Source links:
- Chapter 8 source text supplied for this resource; no additional external source is cited.
3. Q: How does the Law of Similarity operate in online communities?
A: Show answer
Online communities make it easier to locate people who share a specific interest, activity, identity, or goal. Search tools, forums, groups, and specialist platforms allow users to find a much narrower community than they might encounter in an ordinary public setting. Shared interests provide conversation topics and a basis for initial rapport. Similarity, however, should not be confused with trustworthiness. A person who shares a hobby, profession, or cause may still have different values or intentions. Common ground is a starting point for connection, not evidence that a stranger is safe or compatible in every important respect.
Study instruction:
- "Choose a genuine interest and identify how a specialist online group could help you meet people while preserving sensible boundaries."
Key point:
- Shared interests make introductions and early conversation easier.
- Similarity is a basis for rapport, not a substitute for evaluating conduct and credibility.
Source links:
- Chapter 8 source text supplied for this resource; no additional external source is cited.
- eSafety Commissioner: Online Dating Safety
4. Q: What advantage does the large number of people online provide when searching for friends or partners?
A: Show answer
A large online population increases the chance of finding people with uncommon interests, circumstances, or preferences. Someone seeking a niche hobby group or a partner with particular lifestyle priorities is not restricted to the relatively small number of people physically nearby. Search and filtering tools can make this large pool more navigable. The size of the pool also creates exposure to more fake accounts, incompatible contacts, and opportunistic actors. Greater choice therefore increases the importance of screening, patience, and clear personal criteria rather than encouraging rapid trust in the first appealing profile.
Study instruction:
- "List the benefits and risks of a large contact pool and identify screening criteria that are relevant without being discriminatory or intrusive."
Key point:
- Scale increases the likelihood of finding compatible people.
- A larger pool requires more careful screening and does not make individual profiles more reliable.
Source links:
- eSafety Commissioner: Online Dating Safety
- FBI: Romance Scams
5. Q: How can online anonymity reduce the fear of rejection, and what is its limitation?
A: Show answer
A person may feel less exposed when beginning or ending an online interaction because the initial exchange does not require an immediate face-to-face response. This can make it easier to initiate contact, test shared interests, or disengage from an incompatible conversation. Yet anonymity can work in both directions: it protects a cautious user while also allowing another person to misrepresent identity, motives, age, or circumstances. Reduced embarrassment should not be mistaken for reduced risk. Users should remain respectful when ending contact and avoid revealing sensitive information simply because the interaction feels psychologically distant.
Study instruction:
- "Identify one way anonymity can support participation and one way it can enable deception or harmful behavior."
Key point:
- Anonymity can lower the emotional cost of initiating contact.
- The same anonymity can conceal identity and reduce accountability.
Source links:
- eSafety Commissioner: Catfishing
- FTC: Heads Up — Stop, Think, Connect
6. Q: What is prequalification on dating or social platforms, and how effective is it?
A: Show answer
Prequalification is the use of profile descriptions, preferences, filters, and platform settings to narrow the people who can contact or match with a user. It can reduce irrelevant approaches and make the search more manageable. Its effectiveness is limited because users may misstate their characteristics, ignore stated preferences, or bypass platform controls. Filters also measure only the information a person has entered, not their honesty, judgment, or behavior. Treat prequalification as an administrative screening tool. Continue to evaluate consistency, respect for boundaries, and willingness to communicate transparently as the interaction develops.
Study instruction:
- "Review the filters on a platform and distinguish what they can screen from what still requires human judgment."
Key point:
- Filters can reduce unwanted contacts and improve relevance.
- A profile filter cannot authenticate identity or guarantee compatibility.
Source links:
- eSafety Commissioner: Online Dating Safety
- eSafety Commissioner: Catfishing
7. Q: How should someone research a person they are considering meeting online?
A: Show answer
Research should be proportionate, lawful, and focused on checking claims that materially affect safety or trust. A user can compare a person’s stated name, occupation, location, profile history, and photographs with information the person has made publicly available. A reverse image search may reveal that a photograph is associated with another identity. Inconsistencies should prompt neutral questions and further verification, not immediate public accusations. Avoid accessing private accounts, impersonating someone, harassing third parties, or collecting unnecessary sensitive information. Online research is one source of context; it cannot establish a complete picture of a person’s character.
Study instruction:
- "Practice checking a public profile for consistency while respecting privacy, platform rules, and the limits of what public information can prove."
Key point:
- Independent checks can expose reused photographs or inconsistent claims.
- Research should be ethical, proportionate, and treated as evidence to assess rather than a verdict.
Source links:
- FBI: Romance Scams
- eSafety Commissioner: Catfishing
8. Q: How should the benefits of online relationship-building be balanced against its risks?
A: Show answer
Use the Internet as an introduction and communication channel rather than as the sole basis for high-consequence decisions. Benefit from its reach and convenience, but share information gradually, verify important claims independently, keep trusted people informed when appropriate, and move to richer forms of communication before making commitments. Avoid sending money, credentials, identity documents, or intimate material to someone whose identity and intentions have not been reliably established. A healthy online relationship should tolerate reasonable questions and boundaries. If the other person responds to caution with pressure, secrecy, threats, or guilt, pause the interaction and reassess it.
Study instruction:
- "Create a personal set of safeguards for the first stages of an online friendship or romantic relationship."
Key point:
- Digital channels can be useful tools for meeting people.
- Trust should develop through consistent behavior and verification, not through intensity alone.
Source links:
- FBI: Romance Scams
- eSafety Commissioner: Online Dating Safety
- FTC: Heads Up — Stop, Think, Connect
9. Q: What is a digital footprint, and why can it persist after a user deletes a post?
A: Show answer
A digital footprint is the information trail created by online activity, including posts, photographs, comments, messages, account interactions, location data, and information others publish about a person. A user may delete an item from their own profile, but copies may already exist in screenshots, downloads, forwarded messages, backups, archives, or other people’s accounts. Platform retention and legal requirements can also affect how long records remain available. Deletion can reduce visibility, but it cannot guarantee that every copy has disappeared. Users should therefore make sharing decisions on the assumption that material could be retained or redistributed.
Study instruction:
- "Inventory the types of information you publish or generate online and identify which could remain accessible outside your control."
Key point:
- Online activity can create a persistent record beyond the original post or account.
- Deleting content does not guarantee that every copy has been erased.
Source links:
- FTC: Heads Up — Stop, Think, Connect
10. Q: How can the “front-page test” improve online decision-making?
A: Show answer
The front-page test asks whether you would be comfortable if a message, image, comment, or online action became widely visible now or years later. It creates a pause between impulse and publication and encourages the user to consider reputation, relationships, employment, safety, and the privacy of other people. It is not a prediction that every post will become public, nor should it create unnecessary fear about ordinary expression. Its value is as a practical decision rule for sensitive or potentially harmful content. If the answer is uncertain, delay publication, reduce identifying details, or choose a safer communication channel.
Study instruction:
- "Apply the test to a draft post, photograph, or message and decide whether it should be sent, revised, restricted, or withheld."
Key point:
- A short pause can prevent impulsive disclosure.
- Consider the possible future audience before publishing sensitive material.
Source links:
- FTC: Heads Up — Stop, Think, Connect
11. Q: Why should users assume that an online post may reach a wider audience than intended?
A: Show answer
Privacy settings and disappearing-message features can reduce exposure, but they cannot prevent every recipient from taking a screenshot, recording a screen, forwarding a message, or describing the content to others. Content may also be exposed through compromised accounts, shared devices, platform errors, or changes in settings. The intended audience may therefore differ from the actual audience. This is especially important for posts involving personal disputes, work, family members, location, or intimate material. Users should minimize sensitive disclosures and obtain consent before posting images or information about other people.
Study instruction:
- "Review one social account’s audience settings and identify at least two ways a recipient could still redistribute a post."
Key point:
- The audience of a post is not fully controlled by its privacy setting.
- Consent and careful selection of content remain necessary even in restricted groups.
Source links:
- FTC: Heads Up — Stop, Think, Connect
12. Q: What practical steps can reduce an unnecessary digital footprint?
A: Show answer
Share only information that serves a clear purpose, review old public posts, remove unnecessary profile details, restrict location sharing, check photo permissions, and limit the connection of dating profiles to broader social accounts. Review application permissions and disable access to contacts, microphones, cameras, or location when it is not needed. Use separate professional and personal contact details where appropriate, and avoid posting routine schedules or identifying details about home, travel, or family members. These steps reduce exposure but do not create perfect anonymity. Periodically revisit settings because platforms and their defaults can change.
Study instruction:
- "Conduct a privacy review of your profiles, app permissions, location settings, and publicly visible personal details."
Key point:
- Data minimization reduces the amount of information available for misuse.
- Privacy settings should be reviewed periodically and are not a guarantee of secrecy.
Source links:
- FTC: Heads Up — Stop, Think, Connect
- eSafety Commissioner: Online Dating Safety
13. Q: What does proper smartphone etiquette require in shared spaces?
A: Show answer
A considerate user silences notifications where sound would disrupt others, avoids speakerphone conversations in quiet or shared environments, and moves necessary calls to an appropriate place. The user should also consider whether taking a photograph, recording audio, or using a device intrudes on another person’s privacy. The relevant standard is context: a call that is acceptable outdoors may be disruptive in a meeting, cinema, classroom, clinic, or restaurant. Etiquette protects other people’s attention and comfort and signals that the person physically present is being treated as worthy of attention.
Study instruction:
- "Identify three shared settings and specify how phone sounds, calls, recording, and photography should be handled in each."
Key point:
- Device use should fit the setting and the people sharing it.
- Respectful phone behavior protects attention, privacy, and relationships.
Source links:
- Chapter 8 source text supplied for this resource; no additional external source is cited.
14. Q: Why can frequent phone checking damage an in-person relationship?
A: Show answer
Repeatedly checking messages or social feeds during a conversation signals divided attention. The other person may reasonably interpret this as disinterest, impatience, or a lower priority, even if the device use is not intended as disrespect. It also interrupts listening and makes it easier to miss emotional or factual details. A practical response is to silence the device, put it away, and explain briefly if an urgent call must be taken. For meetings or dates, agree on reasonable expectations rather than assuming that everyone has the same tolerance for interruptions.
Study instruction:
- "Observe a conversation in which one person repeatedly checks a phone and identify the effect on listening and rapport."
Key point:
- Attention is a visible form of respect.
- Virtual contact should not routinely displace the person physically present.
Source links:
- FTC: Heads Up — Stop, Think, Connect
15. Q: What privacy information can be revealed by smartphone use beyond the content of a message?
A: Show answer
A device can expose more than the words a user sends. Call logs, contact lists, location permissions, photo metadata, account synchronization, cloud backups, and notification previews may reveal relationships, routines, places visited, or other personal details. A photograph can contain location metadata or recognizable background features. A billing record may also reveal communication patterns to someone with authorized access to the account. Users should secure devices, review app permissions, control location access, and understand what information is stored by service providers. The precise records available depend on the device, service, settings, and applicable law.
Study instruction:
- "Review the permissions and metadata settings on a phone and identify what information each application can access."
Key point:
- Digital privacy includes metadata and usage patterns, not only message content.
- Device and account settings should be reviewed as part of personal privacy management.
Source links:
- FTC: Heads Up — Stop, Think, Connect
16. Q: What risks arise from creating or sharing intimate images and messages?
A: Show answer
Intimate content can be copied, forwarded, stolen, or used for coercion even when it was originally shared in a consensual relationship. A recipient may retain the material after a relationship ends, and an account or device may be compromised. Non-consensual sharing, threats to publish, and digitally altered intimate images can cause serious personal harm. Consent to create or send an image is not consent to redistribute it. The safest decision is not to send intimate content under pressure. If abuse occurs, preserve relevant evidence, report the content to the platform and appropriate authorities, and seek trusted support rather than negotiating with a blackmailer.
Study instruction:
- "Learn the difference between consent to create, consent to receive, and consent to redistribute intimate material."
Key point:
- Consent is specific and does not transfer automatically to later sharing.
- Threats involving intimate images are abuse and should be addressed through support and reporting channels.
Source links:
- FTC: Image-Based Abuse — What to Know and Do
- FTC: Heads Up — Stop, Think, Connect
17. Q: How formal should an email be compared with a text message?
A: Show answer
Email usually creates a more durable and structured record than a casual text, so its tone should reflect the recipient, purpose, and consequences. Messages to employers, clients, schools, or professional contacts should use a clear subject, appropriate greeting, complete sentences, accurate names, and a concise explanation of the requested action. Informal language may be suitable with close colleagues or friends when the relationship supports it. The sender should avoid assuming that a familiar style is appropriate for every audience. A final review for clarity, spelling, tone, attachments, and recipient accuracy reduces preventable misunderstandings.
Study instruction:
- "Rewrite one informal message for a professional recipient while preserving its meaning and making the requested action explicit."
Key point:
- Email formality should match the relationship and the importance of the message.
- Clear, accurate writing reduces misunderstanding and protects professional credibility.
Source links:
- Chapter 8 source text supplied for this resource; no additional external source is cited.
18. Q: Why should a person choose an appropriate email address or screen name for professional communication?
A: Show answer
An email address or screen name is often part of a first impression and may be displayed in recruitment, education, business, or public-service correspondence. A playful alias used among friends may appear unprofessional or create uncertainty when contacting an employer or institution. A neutral address based on a recognizable name is usually easier to identify and manage. Users should also avoid putting unnecessary personal information into an address or public handle. Professional presentation does not require concealing identity; it requires making communication clear, credible, and suitable for its audience.
Study instruction:
- "Assess whether your public-facing email address and screen names are suitable for work, education, and personal use."
Key point:
- A screen name can influence how a message is received before it is read.
- Use recognizable, context-appropriate identifiers without exposing unnecessary personal details.
Source links:
- Chapter 8 source text supplied for this resource; no additional external source is cited.
19. Q: How can capitalization, punctuation, and missing context distort the tone of an email?
A: Show answer
Written messages lack many vocal and facial cues, so readers infer tone from wording, punctuation, formatting, and context. Entire sentences in capital letters can be interpreted as shouting. Very short replies may seem dismissive even when the sender is simply busy. Sarcasm and humor can be misread, especially between people who do not know each other well. The sender should use neutral wording, explain the purpose, and reread the message from the recipient’s perspective. When a subject is emotionally sensitive or easily misunderstood, a conversation by phone or video may be more suitable than a brief email.
Study instruction:
- "Review three messages for wording that could be interpreted differently from the sender’s intention and revise them for clarity."
Key point:
- Digital writing provides fewer contextual cues than face-to-face conversation.
- Neutral language and suitable channel selection reduce avoidable misinterpretation.
Source links:
- FTC: Heads Up — Stop, Think, Connect
20. Q: Why should an angry or distressed person delay sending an important email?
A: Show answer
Strong emotion can narrow attention and encourage accusatory wording, exaggeration, threats, or statements that the sender later regrets. An email also creates a record that can be forwarded or reviewed after the immediate emotion has passed. Drafting without sending can help organize thoughts, but the sender should wait until calm before deciding whether the message is necessary. On review, remove personal attacks, distinguish facts from interpretations, state the desired outcome, and consider whether a direct conversation is better. A delay should not be used to postpone urgent safety reporting or time-critical protective action.
Study instruction:
- "Use a cooling-off process: draft, wait, reread, verify facts, revise, and then decide whether email is the right channel."
Key point:
- Emotional urgency is not always a reason for immediate transmission.
- A deliberate review can prevent escalation and preserve a useful written record.
Source links:
- Chapter 8 source text supplied for this resource; no additional external source is cited.
21. Q: What should be checked before sending an email to multiple recipients?
A: Show answer
Check the To, Cc, and Bcc fields, confirm that each recipient has a legitimate need to receive the information, and review whether the message contains personal, confidential, or sensitive material. “Reply all” can disclose information to a wider group than intended or create unnecessary messages. If a response concerns only one person, reply directly. If recipients need a shared update, use the appropriate group channel and avoid exposing addresses where privacy matters. Before sending, verify attachments and links as well as the subject and message body.
Study instruction:
- "Practice reviewing recipient fields and deciding when To, Cc, Bcc, or a direct reply is appropriate."
Key point:
- Recipient errors can expose information and damage trust.
- Use the minimum audience necessary for the communication purpose.
Source links:
- FTC: Heads Up — Stop, Think, Connect
22. Q: Does deleting an email guarantee that it has disappeared permanently?
A: Show answer
No. Deleting a message from a mailbox may remove the user’s visible copy without removing copies held by recipients, mail servers, backups, archives, legal-hold systems, or synchronized devices. The exact retention and recovery arrangements vary by provider and organization. A sender should therefore treat email as potentially durable and avoid transmitting material that would be harmful or inappropriate if retained or forwarded. Organizations should follow their records-retention, privacy, and legal-hold policies rather than relying on an individual user’s delete command to control the official record.
Study instruction:
- "Compare deletion from a personal mailbox with deletion from recipients’ accounts, backups, archives, and organizational retention systems."
Key point:
- Mailbox deletion is not proof of complete erasure.
- Sensitive communication should be handled according to retention and confidentiality requirements.
Source links:
- FTC: Heads Up — Stop, Think, Connect
23. Q: How can users reduce the risk of malicious email attachments and links?
A: Show answer
Treat unexpected attachments and links as untrusted, even when the sender’s display name looks familiar. An account may have been compromised or the sender address spoofed. Verify the request through a separate channel using contact details already known to be genuine; do not use the phone number or link supplied in the suspicious message. Avoid opening files or enabling macros merely because a message claims urgency. Keep devices, browsers, and security software updated, and report suspicious messages through the provider or organization’s process. A familiar sender name is not authentication of the message.
Study instruction:
- "Create a verification routine for unexpected attachments, payment requests, password resets, and urgent links."
Key point:
- Unexpected links and files can deliver malware or steal information.
- Verify independently before opening, paying, or entering credentials.
Source links:
- FTC: Phishing Scams Can Be Hard to Spot
- FTC: Heads Up — Stop, Think, Connect
24. Q: Why should users understand social-network privacy settings rather than relying on default settings?
A: Show answer
Platforms differ in how they control profile visibility, searchability, tagging, location sharing, contact discovery, and access to posts. Default settings may permit a broader audience or more data collection than a user expects. Review the current settings for each account, check who can view posts and personal details, limit tagging and location permissions, and remove connections or applications that no longer need access. Settings can change after product updates, and another person can still copy visible content. Privacy controls reduce exposure but cannot guarantee that information remains confidential.
Study instruction:
- "Review privacy, tagging, location, search, and third-party application settings on one social platform."
Key point:
- Privacy controls differ by platform and may change over time.
- Settings reduce exposure but cannot prevent every form of copying or redistribution.
Source links:
- FTC: Heads Up — Stop, Think, Connect
- eSafety Commissioner: Online Dating Safety
25. Q: What should guide decisions about accepting online friend or follower requests?
A: Show answer
A request should be evaluated in light of the person’s identity, relationship to the user, reason for contact, and the information the connection would expose. A large number of mutual contacts or an attractive profile is not proof of authenticity. Be cautious with newly created accounts, inconsistent histories, unusual requests for personal information, and people who quickly seek secrecy or move the conversation to less protected channels. It is acceptable to decline or delay a request. Users should not feel obligated to grant access simply because someone is persistent or claims to know them.
Study instruction:
- "Develop criteria for accepting a connection and identify what profile information should remain private from unfamiliar accounts."
Key point:
- A connection request is a request for access, not a social obligation.
- Verify identity and purpose before expanding someone’s access to personal information.
